\section*{How to use this packet}
For each source claim, compare the exact source excerpts with the expanded
PaperInterface specification. Mark up this PDF or fill the blank reviewer box in the TeX source;
return the annotated file for reconciliation into the paper-local human-review
log.

\section*{Open the interactive dashboard}
The interactive dashboard is an optional alternative to using this PDF; it
presents the same review surface rather than an additional review requirement.
After forking and cloning (or pulling) AppliedModelingLib, run the following from the
repository root. The cache command is needed only the first time in a new clone.
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
cd <your-repository-checkout>
lake exe cache get
python3 scripts/review_dashboard.py --paper FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking --serve
\end{ReviewVerbatim}
Open \url{http://127.0.0.1:8765/} in a browser and keep that terminal running
while reviewing. The dashboard records saved annotations in this paper's local
reviewer-owned review record.

  if hstop :
      ∃ time ∈ Finset.Icc 1 count,
        FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveConfidenceRadius time delta <
          |FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCenteredEstimate observation time outcome -

    Nat.find hstop
  else count
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}opt\allowbreak{}Maximize\allowbreak{}Final\allowbreak{}Tail\allowbreak{}Algorithm3\allowbreak{}Source\allowbreak{}Budget}
\textbf{Declaration location:} paper-local
\paragraph{Lean declaration body}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
type:
{Arm : Type u_1} → [Fintype Arm] → Finset Arm → ℝ → ℝ → ℝ → ℝ → ℝ

value:
fun {Arm : Type u_1} [Fintype Arm] (candidates : Finset Arm) (lower upper epsilon delta : ℝ) =>
  if candidates.Nonempty then
    ↑candidates.card * (2 / (upper - lower) ^ 2 * (Real.log (8 / delta) + Real.log ↑(Fintype.card Arm)) + 1) +
      ↑(candidates.card - 1) * (2 / epsilon ^ 2 * (Real.log (8 / delta) + Real.log ↑candidates.card) + 1)
  else 0
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

fun {Arm : Type u_1} [DecidableEq Arm] (roundCount cutoff : ℕ) (lower upper delta : ℝ)
    (trace : Fin roundCount → Finset Arm) =>
  ∑ round : Fin roundCount,
    if (trace round).card ≤ 2 * cutoff then 0
    else
      (trace round).card *
        FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.fixedSampleBudget lower upper
          (FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptivePruneRoundDelta delta ↑round)
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

fun (n : ℕ) (mu : ℝ) (i j : Fin n) =>
  if ↑i < ↑j then FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.theorem7ForwardGap n mu i j

\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\paragraph{Lean declaration body}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
type:
{n : ℕ} → FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.unorderedComparisonCoordinate (Fin n) → Fin n × Fin n

value:
fun {n : ℕ} (coordinate : FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.unorderedComparisonCoordinate (Fin n)) =>
  let arms : Finset (Fin n) := (↑coordinate).toFinset;
  have hcard : arms.card = 2 := FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.theorem7CanonicalEndpoints._proof_2 coordinate;
  have hnonempty : arms.Nonempty :=
    FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.theorem7CanonicalEndpoints._proof_3 coordinate hcard;
  (arms.min' hnonempty, arms.max' hnonempty)
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Absolute\allowbreak{}Maximum}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Absolute\allowbreak{}Maximum\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 54-\allowbreak{}59\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
If pi,j ≥ 12 , we say that i is preferable to j and write i ≥ j. Element i is maximal in a model if i ≥ j
for all j ≠ i. And a permutation `1 , . . . ,`n is a ranking if `i ≥ `j for all i ≤ j. Observe that the
first element of any ranking is always maximal. For example, for n = 3, p1,2 = 1/2, p1,3 = 1/3, and
p2,3 = 2/3, we have 1 ≥ 2, 2 ≥ 1, 3 ≥ 1, and 2 ≥ 3. Hence 2 is the unique maximum, and 2,3,1 is
the unique ranking. We seek algorithms that without knowing the underlying model, use pairwise
comparisons to find a maximal element and a ranking.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

value:

  ∀ (competitor : Arm), 0 ≤ preferenceGap selected competitor
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Borda\allowbreak{}Win\allowbreak{}Probabilities}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Borda\allowbreak{}Win\allowbreak{}Probabilities\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 48-\allowbreak{}51\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Consider a set of n elements, without loss of generality [n] = {1, 2, . . . , n}. A probabilistic model,
or model for short, is an assignment of a preference probability pi,j ∈ [0, 1] for every i ≠ j ∈
[n], reflecting the probability that i is preferred when compared with j. We assume that repeated
comparisons are independent and that there are no “draws”, hence pj,i = 1 − pi,j .
\end{ReviewVerbatim}



\paragraph{Lean-expanded paper semantic target}

value:

  ∀ (arm opponent : Arm), 0 ≤ winProbability arm opponent ∧ winProbability arm opponent ≤ 1
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Consider a set of n elements, without loss of generality [n] = {1, 2, . . . , n}. A probabilistic model,
or model for short, is an assignment of a preference probability pi,j ∈ [0, 1] for every i ≠ j ∈
[n], reflecting the probability that i is preferred when compared with j. We assume that repeated
comparisons are independent and that there are no “draws”, hence pj,i = 1 − pi,j .
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

  ∀ (challenger incumbent : Arm),

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}borda\allowbreak{}Score}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}borda\allowbreak{}Score\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 112-\allowbreak{}117\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
score s(i) = n1 ∑j pi,j is the probability that i is preferable to another, randomly selected, element.
Element i is Borda maximal if s(i) = maxj s(j), and ε-Borda maximal if s(i) ≥ maxj s(j) − ε. A
PAC Borda-maxing algorithm outputs an ε-Borda maximal element WHP (with probability ≥ 1 − δ).
Similarly, a Borda ranking is a permutation i1 , . . . ,in such that for all 1 ≤ j ≤ n − 1, s(ij ) ≥ s(ij+1 ).
An ε-Borda ranking is a permutation where for all 1 ≤ j ≤ k ≤ n, s(ij ) ≥ s(ik ) − ε. A PAC
Borda-ranking algorithm outputs an ε-Borda ranking WHP.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Epsilon\allowbreak{}Maximum}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Epsilon\allowbreak{}Maximum\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 65-\allowbreak{}72\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Let p̃i,j = pi,j − 12 be the centered preference probability. Note that p̃i,j ≥ 0 iff i is preferable to
j. If p̃i,j ≥ −ε we say that i is ε-preferable to j. For 0 < ε < 1/2, an element i ∈ [n] is ε-maximum
if it is ε-preferable to all other elements, namely, p̃i,j ≥ −ε ∀j ≠ i. Given ε > 0, 12 ≥ δ > 0, a
PAC maxing algorithm must output an ε-maxima with probability ≥ 1 − δ, henceforth abbreviated
with high probability (WHP). Similarly, a permutation `1 , . . . ,`n of {1, . . . ,n} is an ε-ranking if `i
is ε-preferable to `j for all i ≤ j, and a PAC ranking algorithm must output an ε-ranking WHP. Note
that in this paper, we consider δ ≤ 21 , the more practical regime. For larger values of δ, one can use
our algorithms with δ = 12 .
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

  AppliedModelingLib.PreferenceRL.ApproximatePreferenceWinner preferenceGap selected epsilon
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\par}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Preference\allowbreak{}Ranking}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Preference\allowbreak{}Ranking\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 54-\allowbreak{}59\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
If pi,j ≥ 12 , we say that i is preferable to j and write i ≥ j. Element i is maximal in a model if i ≥ j
for all j ≠ i. And a permutation `1 , . . . ,`n is a ranking if `i ≥ `j for all i ≤ j. Observe that the
first element of any ranking is always maximal. For example, for n = 3, p1,2 = 1/2, p1,3 = 1/3, and
p2,3 = 2/3, we have 1 ≥ 2, 2 ≥ 1, 3 ≥ 1, and 2 ≥ 3. Hence 2 is the unique maximum, and 2,3,1 is
the unique ranking. We seek algorithms that without knowing the underlying model, use pairwise
comparisons to find a maximal element and a ranking.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

    ∀ (first second : Fin (Fintype.card Arm)), ↑first ≤ ↑second → 0 ≤ preferenceGap (ranking first) (ranking second)
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

\subsection*{\small\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Strong\allowbreak{}Stochastic\allowbreak{}Transitivity}
\noindent\textbf{Paper-local declaration:}\par
{\footnotesize\ttfamily\raggedright Falahatgar\allowbreak{}Et\allowbreak{}Al2017\allowbreak{}Maxing\allowbreak{}Ranking.\allowbreak{}Strong\allowbreak{}Stochastic\allowbreak{}Transitivity\par}
\textbf{Source locator:} {\footnotesize\raggedright cited publication, lines 88-\allowbreak{}93\par}
\paragraph{Verbatim paper-source connection}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
But significantly more general, and more realistic, non-parametric, models may also have max-
ima and rankings. A model is strongly stochastically transitive (SST), if i ≥ j and j ≥ k imply
pi,k ≥ max(pi,j , pj,k ). By simple induction, every SST model has a maximum element and a rank-
ing. And one additional property, that is perhaps more difficult to justify, has proved helpful in
constructing maxing and sorting PAC algorithms. A tournament satisfies the stochastic triangle
inequality if i ≥ j and j ≥ k imply that p̃i,k ≤ p̃i,j + p̃j,k .
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

value:

  ∀ (first middle last : Arm),
    0 ≤ preferenceGap first middle →
      0 ≤ preferenceGap middle last →
        max (preferenceGap first middle) (preferenceGap middle last) ≤ preferenceGap first last
\end{ReviewVerbatim}


\paragraph{Recorded source-to-declaration screening}
\textbf{Verdict:} \texttt{matches}
\\
{\raggedright
\textbf{Reason:} The exact declaration and Lean expansion preserve both preference premises and the source maximum-\allowbreak{}gap SST conclusion.\allowbreak{}
\par}

fun {Ω : Type u_1} (observation : ℕ → Ω → ℝ) (count : ℕ) (lower upper delta : ℝ) (outcome : Ω) =>
  if hstop :
      ∃ time ∈ Finset.Icc 1 count,
        FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveConfidenceRadius time delta <
          |FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCenteredEstimate observation time outcome -
              FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCompareMidpoint lower upper| then
    if
        FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCenteredEstimate observation (Nat.find hstop) outcome ≤
          FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCompareMidpoint lower upper then
      FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.CompareDecision.lower
    else FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.CompareDecision.upper
  else
    if
        FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCenteredEstimate observation count outcome ≤
          FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.adaptiveCompareMidpoint lower upper then
      FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.CompareDecision.lower
    else FalahatgarEtAl2017MaxingRanking.CompareDecision.upper

[Next verbatim source excerpt]

Lemma 20. S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING(S, ε, δ) uses O( nε2 log nδ ) comparisons and w.p.≥ 1 − δ
returns an ε-ranking.

Proof. S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING calls E STIMATE -P ROBABILITY for O(n2 ) times, once for each
pair and each EP (i, j, ε/2, δ/n2 ) uses O( ε12 log nδ ) comparisons and hence bound on comparisons follow.


                                                      17

[form-feed in source extraction]
W.p.≥ 1 − δ/n2 , E STIMATE -P ROBABILITY(i, j, ε/2, δ/n2 ) approximates pi,j with p̂i,j such that ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤
 ε
 2
   . By the union bound, w.p.≥ 1 − δ, ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤ 2ε ∀i, j, ∈ S. From here we assume that ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤ 2ε
∀i, j, ∈ S and show that the output is an ε-ranking. Let S t denote the set of remaining elements in S after
t elements are removed from S. We first show that for 0 ≤ t ≤ n − 1, there is one element e such that
p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε ∀j ∈ S t and such an element is an ε-maximum of S t . Observe that if there is an element e such
that p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε/2 ∀j ∈ S t then pe,j ≥ 12 − ε ∀j ∈ S t and hence e is an ε-maximum of S t . Further recall
that for the absolute maximum at∗ of S t , pat∗ ,j ≥ 21 ∀j ∈ S t and hence p̂at∗ ,j ≥ 12 − ε/2 ∀j ∈ S t . Therefore
there will be at least one element e s.t. p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε/2 and such an element will be an ε-maximum of S t .
S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING deletes one such element from S t and adds it to the end of the ordered
output set. Since for every t, S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING adds an ε-maximum of S t to the output set,
the Lemma follows.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

Lemma 20. S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING(S, ε, δ) uses O( nε2 log nδ ) comparisons and w.p.≥ 1 − δ
returns an ε-ranking.

Proof. S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING calls E STIMATE -P ROBABILITY for O(n2 ) times, once for each
pair and each EP (i, j, ε/2, δ/n2 ) uses O( ε12 log nδ ) comparisons and hence bound on comparisons follow.


                                                      17

[form-feed in source extraction]
W.p.≥ 1 − δ/n2 , E STIMATE -P ROBABILITY(i, j, ε/2, δ/n2 ) approximates pi,j with p̂i,j such that ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤
 ε
 2
   . By the union bound, w.p.≥ 1 − δ, ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤ 2ε ∀i, j, ∈ S. From here we assume that ∣pi,j − p̂i,j ∣ ≤ 2ε
∀i, j, ∈ S and show that the output is an ε-ranking. Let S t denote the set of remaining elements in S after
t elements are removed from S. We first show that for 0 ≤ t ≤ n − 1, there is one element e such that
p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε ∀j ∈ S t and such an element is an ε-maximum of S t . Observe that if there is an element e such
that p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε/2 ∀j ∈ S t then pe,j ≥ 12 − ε ∀j ∈ S t and hence e is an ε-maximum of S t . Further recall
that for the absolute maximum at∗ of S t , pat∗ ,j ≥ 21 ∀j ∈ S t and hence p̂at∗ ,j ≥ 12 − ε/2 ∀j ∈ S t . Therefore
there will be at least one element e s.t. p̂e,j ≥ 21 − ε/2 and such an element will be an ε-maximum of S t .
S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING deletes one such element from S t and adds it to the end of the ordered
output set. Since for every t, S TRONG -T RANSITIVITY-R ANKING adds an ε-maximum of S t to the output set,
the Lemma follows.

\section*{Source claims}
\subsection*{Review row 1}

\paragraph{Verbatim source input}
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
Lemma 1. For εu > εl , C OMPARE(i, j, εl , εu , δ) uses ≤ (εu −ε       l)
                                                                         2 log δ comparisons and if p̃i,j ≤ εl ,

then w.p.≥ 1 − δ, it returns 1, else if p̃i,j ≥ εu , w.p.≥ 1 − δ, it returns 2.

Now we present S EQ -E LIMINATE that uses the testing subroutine C OMPARE and finds an ε-
maximum.

3.1.2   S EQ -E LIMINATE Algorithm

S EQ -E LIMINATE takes a variable set S, selects a random running element r ∈ S and repeatedly
uses C OMPARE(c, r, 0, ε, δ/n) to compare r to a random competing element c ∈ S ∖ r. If C OMPARE
returns 1 i.e., deems p̃c,r ≤ 0, it retains r as the running element and eliminates c from S, but if
C OMPARE returns 2 i.e., deems p̃c,r ≥ ε, it eliminates r from S and updates c as the new running
element.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

stant depends only on c but not on n. Hence it is advantageous to use this method to pick near-
maximum element when n is large.

We now present P RUNE that takes an anchor element as input and prunes the set S using the anchor.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}

                                                2 log δ ) comparisons and outputs a set of size less than

2n′ . Further if a is not an εu -maximum of S then w.p.≥ 1 − 2δ , the output set contains an absolute
maximum element of S.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}


\paragraph{Formalized review target}
The archival source above is not asserted equivalent to this formalized target.
\begin{ReviewVerbatim}
For the finite centered SST Prune model, under the displayed weak cutoff sqrt(6 n log n) <= n', confidence 1/n <= delta with 0 < delta <= 1/2, threshold separation, and good-anchor premises, the source-scheduled stopped execution has probability at least 1-delta/2 of returning at most 2n' arms while using no more than 16 n (2/(epsilon_u-epsilon_l)^2 + 1) (1 + log(1/delta)) comparisons. If the anchor is not an epsilon_u-maximum, the same source-scheduled Prune execution retains an internally chosen absolute maximum with probability at least 1-delta/2.
\end{ReviewVerbatim}
